| 2026-09-07 | Phase 6 Task 9 — the first live window falsified two recorded assumptions | A real 30-poll live window (6,801 events, 02:03–02:34 UTC) run as job `210277303113189`; the failed task's own stack trace read before any fix was proposed; era and lag distributions recomputed locally from the 30 retained landing files; a new Spark test isolating the distinct-late-event case; `make check` 388 passed in 30:39 | **Ingest failed for a real reason, and the fix is narrower than the guard it replaces.** `_reject_non_reduced_era` killed the stream on batch 0: it asserted the live feed was REDUCED_V3-only (a 2026-09-06 claim from a window that happened to contain no old events), and **3 of 6,801 events were `modern_v2`** — including a PullRequestEvent created **2021-07-30**, delivered at an event id adjacent to events created seconds earlier, because GitHub stamps ids on delivery rather than occurrence. The guard was also aimed wrong: its own rationale names `pipeline.dedup`'s **id-less** handling as the hazard, and all three offending events carried valid ids while `pipeline.eras` already handles every era — so it discarded real data to defend an invariant they never threatened. Narrowed to `_reject_id_less_event` (`event_id IS NOT NULL`), with a `non_reduced_count` `observe()` metric so the rate is **reported, not assumed**. **A second and larger finding is left open, not fixed:** a *distinct* event whose event time is behind the 10-minute watermark is counted by `late_event_count` and **absent from Silver** — proven by a new test. The existing test covered only a late *duplicate* and justified the drop as "not data loss" on that basis; that does not transfer, since **18.0% of the window exceeded the watermark and 16.4% carried an event time over a day old**, all first-sightings. It is not losing data today only because the job polls to completion before ingesting, so every file lands in one batch whose watermark has not advanced — **an accident of batching, not a property of the design**; in the continuous shape the code's own docstring calls "Task 9's real shape", roughly one event in six would vanish. Options recorded, decision deferred out of Task 9's gate. `n` is one 31-minute window: enough to prove existence, not to pin either rate. `2026-09-07-live-feed-era-and-watermark.md`. |
| 2026-09-07 | Phase 6 Task 9 — the gate: a live event changes a served online feature value | Two real windows against a purpose-built centralus stack (run `210277303113189`, 30 polls; run `449453318124725`, 5 polls), V1/V2 snapshots read straight out of Lakebase Postgres over psycopg, every count cross-checked against the 35 retained raw JSONL poll files rather than against the pipeline's own account of itself; `terraform plan -destroy` count read before applying; `make check` 388 passed | **Gate met, demonstrated end to end rather than asserted: 84 repos had their served feature values change, 684 were added, 0 lost** — e.g. `repo 1125421191` went `events_prior_24h=11 @02:26:54` to `18 @04:51:02`, a value that could only exist because of events polled 25 minutes after the first was read out of Postgres. **Offline↔online consistency is exact against the raw feed**: 4,925/4,925 distinct `repo_id` and 3,721/3,721 distinct `actor_login`; **zero duplicates across all 6,801 events**, extending Task 1's consecutive-pair result to a whole window. **Freshness decomposed**, which Task 1 explicitly warned must not be reported as one number: end-to-end p50 **561s**, of which GitHub's own feed delay is **302s (54%)** and Almanac's poll→serve is 260s (46%) — and only **121s** of that is pipeline work (ingest 68s, features 35s, publish 18s), the rest being the bounded-window design making the first poll's events wait for the fifth. **Capture 8.5–8.9%** of §12's archive-measured 155–162K/hour, against Task 1's 7.1–7.4%: same order, both `n=1` windows at different times of day, not a revision. **Finding 2 confirmed in production, with the prediction recorded first**: cycle A (fresh checkpoint, watermark at 0) lost **0** repos; cycle B (watermark restored from checkpoint) lost **161** — so the design is correct on a cold start and quietly lossy in steady state, the worst pairing. Spark's `observe()` and an independent count of the raw JSONL agreed on the late-event total to the row (**217 = 217**). **Three more assumptions died on contact with a real run**, each written from an API's shape rather than from an execution: `publish_table` does *not* create its catalog; that catalog must be a **standard** catalog, since a Database Catalog is rejected outright (`MANAGED_ONLINE_CATALOG is not currently supported`); and its schema is not created either. `terraform validate` passed all three. Confirmed in passing: `ALTER COLUMN … SET NOT NULL` **is** accepted by managed Delta, which OSS Delta 4.4.0 refuses — the split Task 6 guessed at. **Torn down**: 16 resources planned, count read first, 0 stray `almanac-lb-*` groups left and the westus3 stack untouched — the separate-state safety property doing exactly its job. Teardown itself hit four traps now written into the module README (blank `terraform output` after a partial destroy presenting as an *auth* error; an external location citing dependents that no longer exist; `force_destroy` being inert until an `apply` writes it to state; and a bare `apply` planning to **recreate** the Lakebase instance). **Deferred honestly**: the idle DBU/hour is still unmeasured — `system.billing.usage` is empty in a new metastore (0 rows total) and lags ~a day in the main one, so it cannot be read during the window it measures; `workspace_id=7405616381250124` was captured before teardown to keep the rows attributable. *(Measured the next day once the rows landed — see the 2026-09-07 closeout row below.)* `2026-09-07-live-feed-era-and-watermark.md`. |
| 2026-09-07 | Phase 6 Task 10 — dedup on write, so late events stop being discarded | Microsoft Learn's stream-deduplication and MERGE pages (both dated 2026-08-24, read 2026-09-07) checked before implementing; the distinct-late-event regression test flipped from asserting the loss to asserting the fix; `test_stream_exactly_once.py` re-run against the changed mechanism rather than assumed to still hold; `make check` | **The 161-repo loss Task 9 measured is closed, and the fix is narrower than "widen the watermark".** `stream_events` no longer applies a watermark at all — the stream is now **stateless**, with no state store to grow — and `write_stream_silver` deduplicates with an **insert-only Delta MERGE on `event_id`**, which is Databricks' own documented pattern for this exact problem. Late events land; only genuine duplicates are suppressed. `watermark` is renamed `late_after` throughout (CLI flag `--late-after-minutes`), because a parameter that no longer watermarks anything is precisely the misleading name the code-style checklist exists to catch. **Two deviations from the documented example, both load-bearing.** The docs bound *both* sides of the merge; copying the **source**-side bound verbatim would have reintroduced the very bug being fixed, since it refuses to insert old events at all. And the docs' wall-clock target bound (`current_date() - N days`) was itself wrong here — **a test caught it, not review**: fixture data dated months earlier fell outside the window, so duplicates sailed through and the assertion failed with `['1','1','2']`. The bound is now the **batch's own `event_date` span**, which is exact rather than heuristic (`event_date` derives from `created_at`, so a duplicate necessarily carries the same one and cannot fall outside it), prunes at least as hard, and removes a wall-clock dependency that would have made replay behave unlike a live window. **Exactly-once changed mechanism and was re-verified, not assumed:** `txnAppId`/`txnVersion` are `DataFrameWriter` options that do not apply to MERGE, so a replayed batch is now genuinely reprocessed and inserts nothing rather than being skipped outright. The test asserts on outcome — an interrupted run's row set against an uninterrupted one — so it still tests the real contract; both cases pass unchanged, as do all 5 replay tests, whose *outcomes* are identical for a better reason (duplicates suppressed because they are duplicates, not because they were late). Order-independence strengthened as a side effect: with no watermark, arrival order cannot affect the result at all. `2026-09-07-live-feed-era-and-watermark.md`. |
| 2026-09-07 | Phase 6 closeout — Task 10 verified against the real failing conditions, and the idle rate finally measured | Task 10's MERGE replayed on an ephemeral job cluster against ADLS-backed Delta with a genuinely cold Silver prefix, using the same 35 poll files that produced the loss (run `444588116034666`); `system.billing.usage` ⋈ `system.billing.list_prices` read for `workspace_id=7405616381250124` once the rows landed, cross-checked against Azure Cost Management by resource group and by service; scratch schema, volumes and Silver prefixes dropped and the drop verified by listing | **The fix holds where the bug actually happened.** Local fixtures cannot reach the failing conditions — the 161-repo loss needed ADLS-backed Delta *and* a restored cloud checkpoint — so it was replayed there: 30 files cold → **6,801 rows**; +5 files on the **resumed** checkpoint → **8,007 rows**, i.e. 6,801 + all 1,206 late events, at 8,007 distinct `event_id`, exactly the step where the watermark path lost 161 repos. `observe()` and the client-side count agreed on 217 late events again. Two harness bugs were mine, and the second is the instructive one: the run "failed" on `distinct repo_id 5771 != 5770` because **Spark counts `NULL` as a distinct value** and 2 of the 8,007 events carry no repo object — the pipeline was right and my assertion was wrong; it now asserts `repoless_rows == 2` explicitly, a stronger check than the one that failed. No Terraform and no Lakebase were re-provisioned: verifying the write path does not require rebuilding the serving path. **The last deferred exit-gate item is closed.** Idle draw at CU_1 is **0.852 DBU/hour → $0.503/hour → $12.06/day** on `PREMIUM_DATABASE_SERVERLESS_COMPUTE_US_CENTRAL` at $0.59/DBU-hour, flat to four decimals across `n = 14` consecutive 10-minute billing buckets. **A stopped instance bills nothing** — the 02:50–03:10 stopped window contains no usage rows at all, not merely smaller ones, which is what makes stopping-before-publish a real saving rather than a plausible one. Whole experiment: **$3.69** in DBUs, of which the store was $1.26 and the *largest* line ($1.62) was the SQL warehouse used to verify it. All-in Azure was **$2.71** across the two `almanac-lb-*` groups, split 49% Databricks / 31% VMs / 20% storage+NAT+VNet — so `2026-09-06`'s "DBUs are ~half of real spend" ratio survives a second, independently provisioned stack. I first wrote that this stack was serverless and therefore exempt; **one query disproved it** (the publish job ran on a classic job cluster, so real VMs behind a real NAT gateway), and the corrected paragraph is in the findings doc. **That measurement also overturned a claim this project had recorded twice.** The rate was in `system.billing.list_prices` all along — `PREMIUM_DATABASE_SERVERLESS_COMPUTE_*` priced since 2025-06-11, ~15 months earlier — and the searches that "proved" otherwise looked for `LAKEBASE`/`POSTGRES`/`ONLINE`/`OLTP`, none of which appear in a SKU name, because **Databricks SKU names are meter names, not product names**. Phase 5's identical finding fails the same check (`PREMIUM_SERVERLESS_REAL_TIME_INFERENCE_US_WEST_3`, priced since 2013-01-01), so the "pattern" built from the two was an `n=2` generalization over two instances of one mistake. Corrected in place at both sites. The $0.26/DBU retail-API figure was also 2× low against Databricks' own catalog ($0.52 same region), so **prefer `list_prices` over the retail API** — it is the meter that actually bills. `2026-09-07-live-feed-era-and-watermark.md`, `2026-09-06-events-api-and-online-store-rates.md` §3, `2026-09-04-phase-5-corpus-and-index-choice.md`. |
| 2026-09-07 | Phase 7 planned — design doc §4.7 and a 14-task implementation plan | Every decision checked against a primary source before recording (Microsoft Learn on Power BI Desktop's platform support and on the UC lineage system tables; the Databricks Terraform provider docs for `databricks_dashboard` and `auto_capture_config`), and every claim about existing state checked against the live workspace rather than against the design doc | **A survey ran before the plan was written, and it changed the plan three times.** §10's "data contract enforced as a CI failure" is **already satisfied for Gold** — dbt `contract: enforced: true` on both consumer models plus `test_gold_contracts.py` proving the build fails — so Task 4 extends contracts to the surfaces with none (features, streaming Silver) instead of rebuilding what Phase 2 shipped. **Two of §7's page-2 panels have no recoverable source** and ship visibly marked rather than faked, the same discipline the Photon A/B used withholding an indeterminate arm. And **one item turned out to be time-critical in a way §9 never suggested**: `almanac-pr-review-sla-risk` is `READY` with `scale_to_zero=true` and drew **zero inference DBUs on 09-07** — it was never torn down, contrary to what I had assumed and stated — but `auto_capture_config` is `null`, so nothing has ever been logged, and the only traffic that can ever be captured is traffic after capture is enabled. Deferring it to Phase 8 would not delay a panel, it would destroy the data, so it is Task 1. *(**Corrected during Task 1**, before anything was applied: the mechanism named here and the one-way constraints stated with it — "payload logging cannot be re-enabled once disabled; catalog/schema/prefix cannot change after setup" — both belong to the **legacy** `auto_capture_config`, whose product documentation is formally retired. The current mechanism is `ai_gateway.inference_table_config`, and its constraint runs the other way: once enabled, the endpoint cannot revert to legacy tables. See the Task 1 row below.)* **Four §4.7 decisions supersede earlier text in the design doc, each saying which.** Power BI Desktop is Windows-only with no Mac version and none planned (Microsoft restated this Sept 2025), so §7's "three Power BI pages" becomes two AI/BI dashboards — which is the better arrangement anyway, since `databricks_dashboard` takes a `file_path` to committed JSON and a `.pbix` is a binary blob no CI can diff — plus one Power BI page keeping §7's non-negotiable limitations panel. **Lineage moves from OpenLineage to Unity Catalog's own, decided against measured state**: `system.access.column_lineage` is already enabled and already holds **18,102 rows spanning 09-02 → 09-07**, Phases 2 through 6, with zero instrumentation ever done. The trap that shapes the task was measured before the task was written — of Almanac's **4,778** lineage rows across 29 sources, **4,133 (86.5%) carry only `source_path`, never `source_table_full_name`**, because Bronze/Silver/features are external Delta paths, so the obvious query filtering on table name would return **13.5% of the graph and report no error**. What UC cannot see (local Spark runs, i.e. the whole test suite; a rolling 1-year retention window) ships *in* the artifact rather than in a footnote. The cloud window narrows to the SQL warehouse only, with the full-stack demo deferred to Phase 8 and **re-provisioning itself becoming a deliverable** carrying Phase 6's four teardown traps as executable checks. Phase 6's absent console evidence is named as a consequence of a recorded teardown decision rather than left as an apparent oversight. §4.6's unresolved-Lakebase-rate bullet also corrected in place, since it is now settled and its `n=2` "pattern" was one error counted twice. |
| 2026-09-07 | Phase 7 Task 1 — inference capture enabled on the live serving endpoint | `terraform fmt -check` + `terraform validate` (the stated gate for a Terraform-only task, per Phase 4 Task 8's precedent; no Python changed); **targeted** `terraform plan` read before applying; 24 real invocations against the model's logged signature; the resulting rows queried for count, distinct ids, status codes and execution time rather than assumed to exist | **Applied: 1 added, 1 changed, 0 destroyed — the endpoint updated in-place, not replaced.** `almanac_dbx.serving_logs.pr_review_sla_risk_payload` now exists and holds **24 rows from 24 distinct `databricks_request_id`s, zero lost and zero `logging_error_codes`**, all `status_code=200`. **The series honestly starts at `2026-09-07T16:45:55.258Z`** and page 2 must not imply history before it. Model execution time: **min 1 ms, p50 2 ms, max 43 ms** — against Phase 4's measured 263.5 ms warm round-trip, so **the model itself is under 1% of a request** and the rest is serving and network overhead; that decomposition is the honest way to present latency, the same discipline Phase 6 applied to freshness. Measured delivery lag **12–15 min** (0 rows at t+12m, 24 at t+15m). **The mechanism in the plan was wrong and was caught before anything was applied.** The plan said `auto_capture_config`; the Databricks Terraform provider still documents that block **with no deprecation marker**, but its product documentation is formally *retired* ("no longer supported") and directs to `ai_gateway.inference_table_config` — **the provider trails the product, so the provider's silence is not evidence**. Second time a gate-2 check has caught a load-bearing API as superseded before design hardened around it (Phase 5's online-store API was the first). That also **corrected the one-way constraint this project had recorded twice**: "payload logging cannot be re-enabled once disabled; catalog/schema/prefix cannot change after setup" describes the *legacy* mechanism. The real constraint runs the other way — once AI Gateway tables are enabled the endpoint **cannot revert to legacy** — and enabling on an endpoint with no inference table configured is explicitly supported, which was this endpoint's exact case. Corrected in §4.7, the plan and the planning row above. **A second claim died to measurement rather than reading**: the docs describe a "fast inference table" for CPU custom-model endpoints, a `_payload` **view** over `_otel_logs` delivering in seconds. What was actually created is a **MANAGED table with no `_otel_logs` beside it**, so the 1-hour best-effort path applies — 12–15 min in practice. The code comment asserting the fast path was corrected to state what was observed. **A bare `terraform plan` was the real hazard: it read `6 to add, 1 to change`.** Five of those creates were Phase 5's and Phase 6's deliberately torn-down infrastructure — the Lakebase instance (~$12.06/day), the Vector Search endpoint (~$6.72/day with no scale-to-zero), its index, the streaming job and its volume — so a bare apply would have silently resurrected roughly **$19/day** of idle spend. Phase 6's teardown notes had already recorded "a bare `apply` plans to recreate the Lakebase instance"; this is that trap met from the other direction, and `-target` is now written into the plan as non-optional rather than as advice. The inference table lives in **its own schema** with `force_destroy = false`: Databricks creates an internal `_checkpoints` volume beside it whose deletion corrupts the table, and a prediction log is the one artifact here that **cannot be re-derived at any price**. Noted for Task 9, found while seeding: the endpoint returns a **boolean**, but §7's page 1 ranks open items *by predicted breach risk*, which needs a probability — a real design question surfaced before the page was built rather than during it. |
| 2026-09-07 | Phase 7 Task 2 — UC column lineage resolved by path, not just by table name | `pytest tests/unit/test_governance_lineage.py` (7 tests), then **the key tests watched fail** against a deliberately name-only implementation before being restored; `ruff check`/`ruff format --check`; `mypy src tests` repo-wide; and the resolution logic run against the **real** 18,102-row lineage graph, not only fixtures; full `make check` 395 passed | **New `almanac.governance.lineage`** — `read_lineage`/`read_table_locations` (the only I/O), `canonicalize`, `column_edges`, `tier_edges`, `tier_of`, all pure `DataFrame -> DataFrame` per CLAUDE.md's structural rule. **Resolution runs path -> name, and the direction is the whole design.** The obvious implementation keyed on `*_table_full_name` would return **13.5%** of this repo's graph and report no error, because 4,133 of its 4,778 rows carry only `source_path` (Bronze, Silver and features are external Delta paths). But canonicalizing the other way — everything to paths — is equally wrong and less obvious: **Gold's tables are *managed*, so their `storage_path` points into `unity-catalog-storage`**, which would collapse every managed Gold table into one meaningless tier. Silver is external and so genuinely appears under both identities; the `information_schema.tables` mapping unifies them. `int_pr_events` has no `storage_path` at all (it is a view), so the fallback chain has to tolerate that too. **The tests were proven to discriminate rather than assumed to**: against a name-only `canonicalize`, **5 of 7 go red**, including the path-only case and the same-table-two-identities case — the 86.5% measurement turned into a regression guard instead of a comment. **First contact with the real graph resolved every medallion tier** — `bronze -> silver` 3,680 column edges, `external -> bronze` 2,376, `silver -> gold` 112, `silver -> features` 31, `gold -> features` 12 — and surfaced a `gold -> features` edge that looked at first like a violation of §3.1's Silver-native feature rule. **It is not, and it was checked before being claimed**: `features/similarity_runner.py:82` reads `fact_pull_request` deliberately, exactly as Phase 5's plan specifies, because `similar_prior_breach_rate` needs prior PRs' labels and the label lives in Gold; `features/spine.py` separately documents that the *spine* is Silver-native. The real finding is narrower and worth keeping: **CLAUDE.md phrases §3.1 absolutely** ("never a read of `fact_pull_request` or `agg_repo_daily`") **while the built system carries one deliberate, documented exception** — a gap between a stated rule and reality, found by the lineage artifact on its first real run, which is exactly the job it exists to do. Noted for Task 14's §10 reconciliation, not a code change. Also surfaced for Task 3: Phase 2's Photon A/B shadow schemas (`ab_photon_*`, `ab_standard_*`) appear as real tiers, so the artifact must decide whether to include them or scope to the production path. |
| 2026-09-07 | Phase 7 Task 3 — the column-lineage artifact, with its blind spots shipped on it | `pytest` on 8 new artifact tests + 11 lineage tests (19); `ruff`/`ruff format`/`mypy src tests` repo-wide (129 files); the artifact generated for real against the live workspace **twice**, the first output rejected on inspection; full `make check` **407 passed in 19:32** | **New `almanac.governance.lineage_runner` + `make lineage`**, writing `docs/lineage/{lineage.md,column-lineage.json}` — a Mermaid tier graph, a counts table, and the blind spots, all generated, none hand-written. **The first generated artifact was honest and nearly useless, and that was the real work.** The taxonomy knew only the four lake containers, so everything else fell into `external` — including the graph's single largest edge, rendering as *"2,256 unknown tables -> bronze"*. Checked rather than guessed: those nodes are `/Volumes/almanac_dbx/burn/...`, the **hourly GH Archive files**. Widening the taxonomy — this catalog's own schemas are tiers (`embeddings`, `serving_logs`, `models`), UC volumes are a `landing` tier — turned a blob into a readable pipeline: `landing -> bronze` 2,256, `bronze -> silver` 40, `silver -> gold` 33, `silver -> features` 29, `gold -> features` 4. **The graph independently confirmed an architecture decision rather than restating it**: `landing -> silver` 40 is Phase 6's streaming path writing Silver directly from the poll landing zone, bypassing Bronze by design. `external` survives as a residual bucket and **the artifact says so**, alongside the invisible local test suite, the rolling 1-year retention window, and the deliberately excluded Photon A/B schemas — a lineage graph reads as complete unless it says otherwise. **The guard is not circular**: CI cannot regenerate this (system tables need a workspace), so the test asserts the *committed* artifact still describes a whole pipeline — every required hop present, no undeclared tier, no `ab_*` leakage, blind spots still shipped. A broken extraction yields a smaller graph rather than an error, which is exactly the silent failure it catches. `databricks-sdk` promoted from a transitive pin to a declared dependency (floored at 0.136.0, the current release per pypi.org's JSON API) — a transitive pin is not a declaration, and regeneration would have broken silently if either Databricks client dropped it. |
| 2026-09-07 | Phase 7 Task 4 — contracts on the feature tier and streaming Silver, proven by breaches that actually fail | 12 new unit tests + 19 new integration tests (**31 passed in 4:24**), four of them deliberate breaches applied for real against the shipped writers rather than asserted to be refusable; `ruff`/`ruff format`/`mypy src tests` repo-wide (132 files); every mechanism choice settled by a probe against `delta-spark 4.4.0` before being coded, not read off a docs page; the duplicate-key claim re-measured on the real 3,997-row Silver fixture after the fix | **New `almanac.contracts`** — `Contract`, `schema_violations`, `enforce`, `check_expressions`, `apply_constraints`, `duplicate_keys` — wired into `write_and_register` (so all five feature tables, batch and streaming, go through one enforcement path) and into `write_stream_silver`. **The plan's two example breaches turned out not to be equivalent, and the interesting one is the breach Delta *accepts*.** A widened type on overwrite Delta rejects by itself, so that half needed no code. An overwrite **missing a column is accepted**: the table keeps the column in its schema and every row's value becomes null — the one failure a reader cannot distinguish from genuinely absent data, and `features/runner.py` overwrites every table on every run. So the pre-write shape check is not belt-and-braces over Delta's enforcement; it covers the case Delta does not, and `test_delta_alone_would_have_accepted_that_drop` holds that finding in place rather than leaving it as a comment nobody rechecks. **Semantic rules go on the tables as Delta CHECK constraints applied by path**, so no metastore is needed and the local suite exercises the real mechanism rather than a stand-in; measured that they survive `mode("overwrite")`, which the whole design leans on since every run overwrites. That **closed a gap §4.6 had recorded and left open**: open-source Delta refuses `ALTER COLUMN ... SET NOT NULL` on a populated table, so `not_null_key_sql` has never run outside Databricks and nothing enforced non-null keys anywhere the tests could see — but Delta accepts `CHECK (key IS NOT NULL)`, which does. One non-obvious trap recorded for anyone writing a new check: **a NULL fails a Delta CHECK**, unlike a SQL-standard CHECK, so every rule on a legitimately-nullable column is written `col IS NULL OR <predicate>` — getting it backwards would reject valid rows on columns the design intends to be null. Uniqueness stays a test assertion because it is not row-local and no constraint can express it. **The contract found a real defect on first contact, which is the argument for the task**: `repo_activity` carried **two rows under its own declared UC primary key** `(repo_id, event_time)` on 2 of 3,997 fixture rows — two events for one repo at the same instant get different running totals from a ROWS frame, and `as_of_join` reads that key while the online store serves the latest row per key, so both were choosing between two rows arbitrarily. Fixed by keeping the row whose totals include every event at that instant; re-measured after the fix at **3,995 rows, 0 duplicate keys**, all five surfaces clean. **One adjacent nondeterminism was found and deliberately not changed**: `stream/features.py` breaks the same tie with `dropDuplicates`, which satisfies the uniqueness contract but picks its survivor arbitrarily (the two rows differ in `secs_since_last_event`) — pre-existing Phase 6 behaviour, not a contract violation, so it is recorded in the findings doc rather than folded into this task's diff. Two process notes: the first integration test was pathologically slow (a function-scoped fixture rebuilt all five tables ~14 times, ~15 minutes) and is now module-scoped at 4:24; and a run that appeared to hang for 99 minutes was **the laptop sleeping between polls** — a thread dump showed a healthy runnable driver and pytest's own clock said 264s, which is why the dump came before the diagnosis. Full measurements: `docs/findings/2026-09-07-delta-contract-enforcement.md`. |
| 2026-09-07 | Phase 7 Task 5 — the data contract and SLA, every number citing its finding | Every figure traced back to the findings doc that measured it and to the code that enforces it, rather than written from memory; the Gold keys and enforcement mechanisms read off `dbt/models/gold/schema.yml`; the no-alerting claim checked against `infra/terraform/` rather than assumed | **New `docs/data-contract.md`**, closing §10's *Documentation* item, which nothing in the repo provided in any form. Eight contracted surfaces with their keys and where each is enforced; freshness, completeness, correctness invariants, reproducibility, retention, and schema stability. **Freshness states its upstream floor beside its headline**: p50 **561 s** end to end, of which **302 s (54%) is GitHub's own feed delay** and only **121 s is pipeline work** (ingest 68 s, features 35 s, publish 18 s) — an SLA that quietly promised to beat GitHub's own publication delay would be a promise about someone else's system. **Two things the plan did not anticipate.** First, the document needed a *What this does not promise* section: a contract listing only guarantees reads as though the gaps are absent, so no availability SLA, no completeness against GitHub (~7–9% of the firehose, from two `n=1` windows that are *the same order*, not a revision of each other), no freshness guarantee on batch surfaces, and no lineage from local runs are stated as plainly as the guarantees. Second, **`n=1` had to be marked inline** rather than left to the cited findings — the freshness distribution rests on one 5-poll cycle, so p95 688 s is labelled as not-a-percentile where a reader will actually see it. Also recorded where it belongs: late events are **kept, not dropped** (18.0% of a window arrives >10 min behind, 16.4% carries an event time >1 day old), so a consumer may legitimately see an arbitrarily old `created_at` appear — correct behaviour, and the thing whose absence cost 161 repos in Phase 6. **One correction caught by checking rather than remembering**: `agg_repo_daily` is keyed on `activity_date`, not `event_date`. **`reduced_v3` is stated as a consumer-facing limit for the first time** — those events are ingestible but not modelable, so the live path yields activity features only and a consumer needing outcomes must use the historical quarter. README's repo-layout map gained `docs/data-contract.md` and `docs/lineage/`, the latter a Task 3 omission fixed here rather than left for Task 14, since a generated artifact nobody can find is not published. |

| 2026-09-07 | Phase 7 — a Task 4 regression caught by the full suite: the three feature tables stopped sharing a Delta version | The full suite run for Task 7's coverage measurement (457 tests, 45:44); each failure then re-run **serially and without coverage** to separate contention from defect; the constraint counts computed directly from `FEATURE_TABLES` rather than inferred from the error; the fix's guards **watched fail** against a reintroduced silent fallback; 27 integration tests green in 14:34 after | **Three failures, and the split mattered more than the count.** One — `test_the_live_silver_table_carries_its_contract` — **passes serially**: `run_streaming_query` fails hard at a 60 s `awaitTermination` budget, and the coverage run had four xdist workers, 2.34× instrumentation overhead and a load average of 23.7. So the three tables' Delta versions now diverge by run, while `_build_feature_frame` pinned **one** `features_version` across all three. **The crash was the lucky case.** When the requested version happens to exist in all three tables, that code reads **three different points in time and raises nothing** — a violation of this project's one governing principle, arriving exactly where CLAUDE.md says it does, invisible in code review. Proven to discriminate rather than assumed to: reintroducing the exact silent fallback (`versions.get(name)`) turns **2 of 5** red. CI gets **its own `coverage` job rather than a flag on the existing test step**: coverage costs **~2.4×** (46:19 against the 19:32 baseline, quiet machine both times), and a separate job runs *alongside* `check` so wall-clock time to a signal is unchanged; `pull_request`-only while the repo is private on the free plan, with the condition marked for removal when it goes public. **One test-harness change came out of this and it is not cosmetic**: four streaming tests failed on `run_streaming_query`'s 60 s `awaitTermination` budget under `-n 4` *plus* instrumentation — all four passing serially in 85 s — so at 2.4× that budget bought ~25 s of real work. Raised to 180 s, with the reasoning that it guards against a *hang*, which is unbounded, and asserts nothing about latency. A flaky gate is worse than no gate. **Three measurements taken while the machine was quiet, because two earlier ones were not**: `make check-fast` is **31 s** end to end (ruff 0.09 s, mypy 1.50 s warm, pytest 29.6 s) — so the per-task gate costs half a minute and mypy is not the cost, correcting a guess made earlier the same day; and mypy **cold** is 68.47 s against 1.50 s warm, a 45× gap CI pays on every run since it caches no `.mypy_cache`. Noted, not acted on. |

Corrected break-even is **1.31 / 1.54 / 1.65** against a **measured 2.297** multiplier (range 2.118–2.394), with `paid_for_itself: False`, `cost_off $0.40` vs `cost_on $0.74` — Photon costs **1.85x for a 1.31x speedup**. | 2026-09-08 | Phase 7 Task 13 — the decision memo and the watermark postmortem, and a leakage bug found while writing them | Read `train.py`'s split path directly rather than trusting §5.3's summary of the model result — which is what found the defect; §4.5 and §5.1 re-read for the stated requirement before claiming a violation; `make check-fast` (292 non-Spark tests, ruff, ruff format, mypy --strict) — no `src/` or `tests/` change since the 498-passed full run | **Two artifacts, and the memo turned out to have a real answer rather than a rehearsed one.** `docs/decision-memo.md` recommends **not yet** — deploy as a human-in-the-loop ranked queue only after one re-measurement, never as automated action — with **two different confidence levels stated on purpose**: *low* that the reported numbers transfer to production, *moderate* that the ranking genuinely beats the baseline. **The reason for the split confidence is a defect this task found**: `train_classifier` uses `train_test_split(frame, test_size=0.3, random_state=42)`, while §4.5 states *"Train/test must be split **temporally**; a random split is itself a leakage bug"* and §5.1 adds that splits must fall on whole-week boundaries because weekday/weekend latency differs sharply. **Neither is implemented, and `as_of_timestamp` is already in the frame** — the column needed to fix it was sitting in the data the whole time. **The precise statement matters and is made precisely**: the *features* are point-in-time correct, `as_of_join` guarantees it, and the leakage suite is not wrong about what it tests — **no feature sees its own future**. What is unsound is the *evaluation*: a random split scores the model on PRs opened earlier than ones it trained on, with entity autocorrelation the largest channel (the same repos and authors on both sides, features strongly autocorrelated over 92 days). So **0.612 PR-AUC is optimistic by an unmeasured margin** — and the *relative* claim survives better than the absolute one, because the baseline was fit on the same split and enjoys the same advantage, making "beats the baseline 2.15x" far more robust than "achieves 0.612". Blocking measurement costs **≈$1 and one ~15-minute job run**, which makes it the highest-value measurement left in the project. **Not fixed here**: it is a Phase 4 change plus a re-scored champion, not a Phase 7 doc task, and freelancing it into Task 13 would be exactly the scope drift CLAUDE.md forbids. **This also fires CLAUDE.md's deferred trigger** for a point-in-time/leakage review skill — "write it the first time a leakage bug actually gets through" — the first time that trigger has had a real incident behind it. `docs/postmortem-watermark-data-loss.md` covers Phase 6's silent 18% drop, and its uncomfortable centre is that **the repo's own test asserted the buggy behaviour and justified it in its assertion message** (*"correct here, since it is a genuine duplicate of poll 1's row, not data loss"*) — true for the fixture, false for the feed, and worse than an untested path because a reviewer is actively told not to worry. Cold checkpoint lost **0**, resumed checkpoint lost **161**, with the **prediction recorded before the confirming run**, and two independent measurements agreeing to the unit (`observe()` `late_events=217`; a hand count of `polled_at - created_at > 600s` in the raw JSONL, 217 of 1,206). What would have caught it, cheapest first: a test whose late row is *distinct* rather than a duplicate — four lines different from the one that endorsed the bug. |

| 2026-09-08 | Phase 7 Task 15 — the split fixed, and fixtures that could not express time | TDD: `temporal_split`'s 7 tests written and **watched fail** before it existed, then **mutation-tested three ways** — reverting to a random split turned 3 red, boundarying on any day instead of Monday turned 2, allowing an empty training side turned 1; both training paths rewired and `train_test_split` removed from the module; `ruff`, `ruff format`, `mypy --strict`; `make check-fast` 299 passed, then the **full** `make check` twice — 505 items | **Task 13's finding is fixed in code, and the full suite caught a second layer that the fast one could not.** `temporal_split` boundaries on a whole calendar week (§5.1 — an arbitrary split point encodes day-of-week, since weekday and weekend review latency differ sharply) and **refuses**, with a message naming the fix, when the frame spans less than two weeks, rather than returning a silent empty test set that would read as a pass. **The first full run came back 5 failed, 500 passed after 53 m 26 s while `make check-fast` was green at 299** — the whole argument for the run-the-whole-suite-before-every-push rule, in one data point. All five were `temporal_split` correctly refusing: three model-integration fixtures pinned every PR — 39, 39 and 59 of them — to the single instant `2025-08-13 09:00`, so there was no week boundary to split on. **A fixture with no time spread cannot express a temporal split at all**; it had never mattered because a random split does not care when anything happened. Fixed with one shared `tests/helpers.opened_at`, spreading PRs over four calendar weeks with the week as `pr_number % 4` rather than monotonic **on purpose** — these fixtures derive the label *from* `pr_number`, so a monotonic spread would put one class wholly on one side and leave `roc_auc_score` nothing to score. Verified by arithmetic before anything slow ran: train 29 / test 10 and train 44 / test 15, both classes on both sides, no test row preceding a train row. **The champion is deliberately not retrained** — the recorded 0.612 PR-AUC is still the random-split number and is optimistic by an unmeasured margin; re-scoring is a cloud run and the first Phase 8 item. §10 moves to **21 done, 9 partly, 2 not done, 1 not assessable**. The checklist this produced is `.claude/skills/almanac-leakage-review/`, whose gate 1 is the table of leakage *axes* — row time, split time, entity overlap, label construction, target definition — and which of them anything actually covers: the suite was green throughout and was **not wrong**, it tested row time and the bug was on split time. Full `make check`: **505 passed in 49 m 43 s**. |
| 2026-09-08 | Phase 7 -- the pseudonymity gate fired on CI, on a repo with nothing leaked | The failure read from `gh run view --log-failed` rather than guessed at; `local_identifiers`' derivation read directly; TDD with **RED watched first** (`TypeError: unexpected keyword argument 'hostname'`); the fix then verified by **simulating the CI host locally** instead of by pushing and hoping; full `make check` before the push | **A false positive, and the check could not have caught it anywhere but CI.** `local_identifiers()` derives what to hunt for from `socket.gethostname()` and `id -un`. GitHub Actions runs as the login **`runner`**, which is also an ordinary word throughout this repo -- job runner, features runner, `runner.py` -- so the identity gate flagged **6 published files** across `CLAUDE.md`, `README.md` and `docs/STATUS.md`. Locally `id -un` is a personal name that matches nothing, so the check passed on every run for the whole of Phase 7. **This was the first time it had ever executed off this machine**: Phase 7 was built locally and pushed once, today. That is `almanac-leakage-review`'s **gate 3 landing on Phase 7's own code** -- *can the fixture even express the failure?* -- and structurally it could not, because the input is read from the host, so a second machine is the only fixture that exists. Fixed by making `hostname`/`login` injectable (the idiom `RestSession` already uses for its clock, so the exclusion is testable without a second machine) and excluding `_SERVICE_ACCOUNTS`, mirroring the module's existing `_NOT_AN_EMAIL` and its stated reason: *a check that cries wolf is a check people learn to skip*. **Discrimination measured, not assumed**: under simulated CI conditions the scan returns **0** findings, while scanning with `runner` still an identifier returns **111** -- so the exclusion does real work and the scanner is intact. Two new tests, one for the exclusion and one asserting a personal login is **still** caught, so the fix cannot swallow the case the check exists for. Full `make check`: **507 passed in 46m41s**. Cost of finding it in CI rather than locally: ~140 minutes of runner time across two jobs. |
| 2026-09-08 | CI ran out of **disk**, not minutes -- and the suite had already passed | GitHub's own annotation on the job read rather than the cause guessed at (`No space left on device`); the `check` job's step list read to establish what had actually succeeded before the failure; the load measured locally (`du` on `.venv`, the uv cache, and pytest's tmp roots) instead of estimated; the workflow re-parsed with `yaml.safe_load` after editing to confirm step order in **both** jobs | **The code was fine and the suite passed.** In run `34263618259` the `check` job's every real step succeeded -- `ruff`, `ruff format`, `mypy --strict`, **the full `pytest` run**, the Silver fixture and the Gold build -- then the job failed in `Post Run setup-uv` with `System.IO.IOException: No space left on device`, writing its own diagnostic log. `coverage` had already lost its runner at 1h14m with *"the hosted runner lost communication... starves it for CPU/Memory"*, which is the same disk exhaustion seen from the other side, and is why that step reads **cancelled** rather than failed. **Measured, not assumed**: `.venv` is **2.0 GB** (torch 553M, pyspark 475M, pyarrow 122M), the local uv cache **6.6 GB** -- restored *and re-saved* by `enable-cache: true`, which is exactly where `check` died -- and pytest leaves **~305 MB per run** with the last 3 roots retained. Against roughly 20 GB free on a hosted runner after its preinstalled image, and a suite that passed at 49m8s on PR #13 and now takes ~1h26m, this is a cliff the project crossed rather than a flake. Fixed by dropping preinstalled toolchains this repo never uses (dotnet, Android, GHC, CodeQL, boost) in **both** jobs, buying back ~25 GB. **Two causes were considered and one rejected on evidence**: Actions minutes were near the account's free cap (canopica alone at 1,973 of 2,000 for September), which produces cancelled jobs too -- but the annotation names disk explicitly, and a minutes cap would not have let `check` finish its entire test run first. The repo going public the same day removes the minutes question independently. |
| 2026-09-08 | Phase 8 planned -- design doc 4.8 and two plan docs, and the planning measurement falsified a published number | Every claim measured before it was written rather than after: GH Archive's publish lag probed backwards from now (**n=12 hours**), hourly composition counted by downloading and parsing real files (**n=5 hours over 4 days, plus one Q3 2025 hour**), hour-9 file size sampled across **14 consecutive days**, and the outlier hour checked for bot concentration before being believed; the sibling project's 2026-08-31 public-flip checklist read for prior art rather than reinvented | **Three findings, one of which corrects a number this repo publishes.** (1) **GH Archive's 2026 firehose is intermittently degraded**, and the same hour-of-day varies **50x** in size across fourteen days -- 1.5 MB on 09-02 against 86.3 MB on 09-06. Composition, not just volume: 2026-09-05 09:00 is 37.3% PullRequestEvent (27,796 events), while 2026-09-08 14:00 is **94% PushEvent** with PullRequestEvent at ~1% (851). So **a window picked by recency lands on the degraded side**, which is why 4.8 decision 2 makes window quality a measured precondition and a degraded window a refusal. (2) **The published -97% PR-volume claim is wrong**, and wrong the same way the 2026 volume claim and the VM SKU claim were -- generalized from **one hour**. One recent hour carried **10,544** opened PRs against the 2025 hour's 6,702, and it was not a bot flood (5,959 distinct actors, 8,133 repos, top actor 6.2%). This is the **third** instance of the same failure mode, which is worth more than the number; correcting it in place is Phase 8 Task 3. (3) **The reduced era replaced `pull_request.merged` with a distinct `action='merged'`** -- present in every 2026 hour sampled (285/457/283), absent from the 2025 hour, whose actions are only `{opened, closed, reopened}`. `payloads.py:150` reads the field, so `pr_merged` is NULL across the whole reduced era while the information sits unread in `payload.action`. That removes the need for REST enrichment to recover `merged`, leaving only `draft` -- which **must not** be enriched from a current-state fetch, because `is_draft` is a feature taken from the opened event and today's value encodes a future action. Deferred as a **ceiling with its upgrade path named**, not as an omission. **Nothing committed to `main`, and the plan is on its own branch**: `phase-8-ship`, branched from the Phase 7 tip rather than committed into the open PR -- the mistake this file already records from Phase 7's own planning commit. No code written; no test run beyond the measurements above. |
| 2026-09-08 | Phase 8 Task 4 -- the spine fan-out fixed at its cause, and the plan's own task order corrected first | TDD with **RED watched** on all four new tests; the fix then **mutation-tested by reverting `spine.py` and `groups.py` to HEAD** and re-running (4 failed, 9 passed), because all four had gone green on their first run and this repo's own Story 50 calls that a question rather than an answer; `ruff`, `ruff format --check`, `mypy --strict` | **The defect was in two places, and fixing either alone still fans out.** `build_pr_opened_spine` and `compute_pr_static` both emitted one row per opened *event*, then joined on `(repo_id, pr_number)` -- so the 25 PRs GH Archive carries two distinct `opened` events for multiplied 2x2 into 4 rows (7,320,196 against a distinct key count of 7,320,121). Fixed as one shared `earliest_opened_events()` in `spine.py`, used by both, per the code-style rule *fix the cause, not the caller*. **A related duplication was collapsed because it caused this**: the "opened" predicate was written inline in `spine.py` *and* as `_opened()` in `groups.py` -- two statements of the one fact whose disagreement is exactly what fans the join out. **Determinism was the subtle part**: earliest wins, but a `created_at` tie would otherwise leave the winner to Spark's row order, breaking *reproducible byte-for-byte from the same Delta version*. Ordering is `(created_at, actor_login)` plus `pr_draft` **when the frame carries it**, with a test that feeds identical rows in both orders and asserts identical output. *(Corrected in place 2026-09-08: this row first read `(created_at, actor_login, pr_draft)` unconditionally, which is what broke `similarity_runner` -- see the row below.)* **The end-to-end test is the one that matters** -- built from raw events through both builders, since a test on either function alone cannot catch a fan-out that needs both. **The plan's task order was wrong and was corrected in place before any code was written**: Task 1 re-scores the champion through `assemble_training_set`, the same builder this fixes, so the original order would have re-scored on data still carrying 25 PRs at 4x weight. Not pushed: the full suite has not run since this change. |
| 2026-09-08 | Phase 8 Task 2 -- `pr_merged` recovered from `action`, which the reduced era has carried unread all along | TDD, **RED watched** on the derivation test; two **control** tests written alongside it that pass before the fix, so they guard rather than measure; then **mutation-tested two ways** and each mutation caught by its own control; fixtures built from the reduced-era payload shape **measured 2026-09-08** (`base, head, id, number, url` and nothing else), not invented; `ruff`, `ruff format --check`, `mypy --strict` | **A live NULL across the entire reduced era, with the information sitting one field away.** The October 2025 reduction dropped `pull_request.merged` and replaced it with a distinct `action='merged'` -- measured present in every 2026 hour sampled (285/457/283) and **absent** from the 2025 hour, whose actions are only `{opened, closed, reopened}`. `payloads.py` read the field, so `pr_merged` was NULL for every reduced-era row. Now `coalesce(field, derived)`, and **the order is load-bearing**: the rich era carries `merged` on a `closed` action, so deriving first would read a real merge as `False` -- mutation 1 swapped the order and the rich-era control failed. **The derivation refuses to guess**: only `merged` -> True and `closed` -> False; every other action stays NULL, because an `opened` event says nothing about whether the PR ever merged and folding it to `False` would fabricate a negative label for every open PR in the era -- mutation 2 did exactly that and the unknown-stays-unknown control failed. That is the same era-bound-null rule `pr_draft` already follows. **This also removes the need for REST enrichment to recover `merged`** (design doc 4.8 decisions 3 and 4), leaving only `draft`, which must not come from a current-state fetch. Not pushed: the full suite has not run since this change. |
| 2026-09-08 | Phase 8 Task 3 -- the stale rate corrected in place, and **my own correction corrected first** | The origin finding opened and its sample counted **before** writing the correction, rather than the familiar story assumed; every other copy of the figure found by grep across `*.md` and annotated, so the correction cannot sit in one file while three others still read the old way | **The interesting part is that the first draft of this correction was wrong.** It called the -97% figure a *third* `n=1` incident, alongside the 2026 volume and VM-SKU claims. Opening `2026-09-01-third-schema-era.md` shows **n=5** 2026 hours (Jun 10, Jul 15, Aug 12, Aug 19, Aug 28) at a consistent **120-265 opened PRs/hour**, and its own text already warns that *"any single hour is a bad basis for a claim"*. The finding is **sound for the window it sampled**. What actually broke is the *extrapolation* in `2026-09-02-second-source.md`: a Jun-Aug observation re-quoted as "~200 opened PRs/hour **on current data**", a standing rate. **The quantity is not stationary** -- re-measured 2026-09-08 at **308, 415, 579 and 10,544** opened PRs/hour, one hour above the 2025 reference of 6,702 -- so **no `n` within one period would have caught it**. That is a genuinely different failure from the two `n=1` incidents, and flattening it into them would have taught the wrong lesson: gate 1 asks whether the sample covers the dimension being generalized over, and *"now"* is a different point on the **time** axis than *"August"*. A rate re-quoted as current needs a fresh reading, not a bigger old one. Corrected in place with the original analysis kept unedited beneath it, per the design-decision skill; the same annotation added to `docs/limitations.md` and design doc S12, where the figure was also standing as current. |
| 2026-09-08 | Phase 8 Task 5 -- window characterization, and a CLI that was a silent no-op | TDD, **RED watched**; thresholds calibrated against four **measured** hours rather than chosen by feel; **mutation-tested four ways**; then run against the **real files** for 2026-09-05 09:00 and 2026-09-08 14:00, not just fixtures; `ruff`, `ruff format`, `mypy --strict` | **The gate works and reproduces the planning measurements exactly**: the healthy hour profiles at 74,595 events / 10,544 opened / 37.3% PR share and is **accepted**; the degraded hour at 87,563 / 308 / 1.0% is **REFUSED**, naming three failed rules. `failed_rules` is a **tuple, not a boolean**, mirroring `quality.py`'s `_failed_rules` for the same reason -- a refusal nobody can analyse by rule is one nobody can act on. **The calibration is binding in both directions**: mutation 4 raised the PR-share floor to 10% and the *rich-2025-reference* test failed, which is the point of having it -- a gate that rejects the window the champion was trained on is miscalibrated, not strict. **Two real defects the work found in itself.** (1) `python -m almanac.extract.characterize` **printed nothing and exited 0 on both windows** -- there was no `__main__` guard, so the module imported and returned. The tests called `main()` directly and passed throughout: they tested the function, not the entry point, which is the same shape as the leakage suite testing one axis. Caught only by running the real command. (2) The first entry point used `raise SystemExit(main())`, which re-introduces the exact bug `almanac.cli.run_cli` exists to prevent -- `sys.exit(0)` marks a Databricks `spark_python_task` FAILED even on success, measured 2026-09-02 on a backfill that had already written all 92 days. Switched to `run_cli`. Both are the code-style skill's *is it already in this codebase?* rung, missed once and then applied. Not pushed: the full suite has not run since. |
| 2026-09-08 | Phase 8 Task 6 -- offline drift, and a mutation that survived | TDD, **RED watched**; **mutation-tested three ways** and **one survived**, which is the finding; the Spark edge tested separately from the pure comparison; `ruff`, `ruff format`, `mypy --strict` | **Section 10's longest-standing *not done* now has code behind it.** Line 1718 asks for drift, line 1911 maps it to the MLOps claim, and line 1922's resume-bullet template already asserts it -- so this was scope the project advertised and had not built. Pure comparison over `FeatureSummary` objects with a thin Spark `summarize` edge, the same split Task 5 uses, so the logic is testable without a SparkSession. **Schema drift is reported instead of covariate drift, never alongside it**: when a field is gone its remaining distribution is an artefact, and a PSI over a dead column is noise dressed as a measurement. `is_draft` is the real case -- populated on every rich-era PR (13,301 in the 2025 hour) and **null on every reduced-era row** -- and its stated response is concrete: *the champion reads this feature, so it cannot score this window; refuse to serve rather than re-baselining.* An alarm without a stated response is theatre. **The mutation that survived is the useful part**: replacing the bin union with an intersection changed nothing, because every existing test spelled a vanished category as an explicit `0.0` rather than omitting the key. Intersecting silently drops a category that disappeared entirely -- scoring **the worst possible drift as milder than a small shift**. Fixed by a test asserting the two spellings score identically; the mutation now fails. **PSI thresholds are labelled as convention, not measurement** (0.1 moderate, 0.25 major), because they come from practice rather than from anything this project measured, and this repo's rule is that a number names where it came from. | 2026-09-09 | Phase 8 close-out -- the public surface, and a licence the repo did not have | The sibling project's public README read rather than reasoned about, after the user asked what had been done there; Codecov's **own pricing page** and tokenless-upload docs checked instead of assumed; the `codecov-action` version taken from its releases API; every new README link resolved; every number in the profile README grepped against this repo before pushing | **The most consequential gap was not a badge, it was a missing licence.** Canopica ships MIT; Almanac shipped **none**, which on a public repo means all rights reserved -- nobody can legally fork or reuse a portfolio piece. MIT added verbatim from the sibling so both carry identical terms. **The badge work began with a wrong call and was corrected**: a hardcoded `88%` was replaced with a *gate* badge to avoid staleness, which broke the pattern Canopica had already set (hardcoded percentages, CI pinned to `?branch=main`) -- the user caught the inconsistency. Resolved by matching the sibling **and** keeping the gate as a second badge, then replaced entirely by **Codecov**, which is dynamic and settles the staleness argument properly. **Two things that would have made Codecov silently useless, both found before wiring**: the coverage job was `pull_request`-only, so nothing would ever have produced a number on `main` -- and a Codecov badge reads the **default branch**, so it would have rendered *unknown* forever, the dynamic equivalent of the stale number it replaced; and tokenless upload covers only fork -> public-upstream PRs, so a public repo still needs `CODECOV_TOKEN` (their docs, not the assumption that public implies tokenless). The upload step is **guarded on the token existing** rather than `fail_ci_if_error: false`, because a step that silently no-ops is the exact failure this repo keeps writing postmortems about -- absent token skips visibly, present token fails the build on a bad upload. `codecov.yml` mirrors `fail_under = 85` rather than inventing a second threshold, and marks patch coverage **informational**, since a phase here lands dbt and Terraform beside Python and failing a PR over an untested `.sql` file trains people to ignore the check. The semantic change is real and visible in the numbers -- `merged` moved from a *field* to an *action value*, which is exactly why `event_action`'s null share collapses **74.6% -> 0.3%** -- but a PSI cannot tell *the distribution moved* from *the meaning moved*. | 2026-09-10 | Phase 9 Task 8 -- the tool gateway: allow-list, read-only, audit | TDD with **RED watched** (`ModuleNotFoundError`, no `almanac.agent.gateway`, before the module existed); **mutation-tested ten ways**, all ten killed against an explicitly recorded green 11-test baseline; `make check-fast` -- lint, `mypy --strict`, **383 passed** | **Every call to the tool surface goes through one broker, or the audit log is a lie.** `allow_list` is **two independent gates**: a name must be on a reviewed constant *and* the server must declare it read-only. The reviewed list is the security property -- a tool the server gains, of any kind, cannot be called until someone edits a constant, which is a change a reviewer sees; the read-only check is the consistency one, catching a reviewed tool that quietly lost its annotation, and it **fails the build rather than serving the rest**, since silently narrowing the surface is how a capability disappears without anyone noticing. **Neither gate is a claim about what a tool's body does**, and the module says so: the gateway cannot know that, and the plan is right that grepping tool source for `write` would be theatre. What it guarantees is that nothing outside the reviewed four ever runs -- which the central test proves by registering a **write-capable tool the real server has no way to construct**, then asserting its body never ran. **One deliberate departure from the plan's wording.** The plan says a write tool must be "refused rather than logged"; the refusal *is* logged, as an `outcome="refused"` record with no result, because a security log in which the most interesting event leaves no trace is the failure this log exists to make visible. The guarded property is unchanged and is what the test asserts: the tool body never ran and no `ok` record exists. **The audit log is append-only by construction** -- the only write opens in append mode, JSON Lines so a crash costs the last call and not the session, and `w` in place of `a` turns three tests red. **`ToolRefusedError` and `schemas.Refusal` are two different events and the log keeps them apart**: a reduced-era `predict` through the gateway is a **completed call** carrying a refusal payload, not a gateway refusal, and a mutation that conflates them is killed -- Phase 10 reads this log, and a verifier that cannot tell "the data was missing" from "the tool was never allowed to run" would score both the same. `StubScoringModel` and `StubRegistry` move into `tests/agent_fixtures.py` at their second user; the parameterized registry stub in the `versions` tests stays where it is, because it varies the MLflow tag to test parsing and is a different fact, not a third copy.
